# Porting ledger

This file tracks which upstream iOS files have been ported to Kotlin, where each one lives
in this project, and every place the port deliberately differs from upstream.

Upstream is [`perezjuanj/OpenCircuit`](https://github.com/perezjuanj/OpenCircuit) (MIT,
© 2026 Juan Perez). The commit the port currently tracks is recorded in
[`UPSTREAM.md`](UPSTREAM.md); that file is the only source of the pin. The `SHA` column
below records the upstream commit each row was ported from.

## Porting rule

- Port each upstream Swift source file **together with its test file**. The ported test is
  run and shown failing before the Kotlin source exists, then passing after.
- A module is done only when its ported upstream test vectors pass.
- The protocol reference is upstream's [`docs/PROTOCOL.md`](../docs/PROTOCOL.md). Where it
  disagrees with the Swift code, the protocol document wins.
- Byte values are `Int` 0–255 read through one unsigned helper; byte sequences are
  `ByteArray`; time is `java.time.Instant`. Kotlin unsigned types are not used.

Paths: upstream `S/` = `ios/OpenCircuitKit/Sources/OpenCircuitKit/`, `T/` =
`ios/OpenCircuitKit/Tests/OpenCircuitKitTests/`. Kotlin `main/` and `test/` =
`ringkit/src/{main,test}/kotlin/io/github/opencircuit/ringkit/`.

Every staging pass changed at least one night when switched off (cadence 11, leading-run exemption 10, wear gate 11, rescue 9, widen 3, offset 68 nights). Then 12 daytime nap days in 6 shapes (blocks either side of the 15 min floor and the 3 h mark, activity-tagged desk stillness, a nap after the night, a restless nap, short uniform naps) and 30 synthetic `0x48` PPG bursts in 6 shapes (clean, desaturating, low perfusion, noisy, a re-dumped previous-night backlog with duplicate frames, too short for one window). The test compares every line whole and exactly, and doubles within 1e-9 with every non-bit-identical value listed; nights render on 4 threads and are compared in order: 431 nights, 32150 lines, 0 of 8731 doubles differ (2.6 s). Lines an owner-approved improvement moves are named, night by night and line kind by line kind, in the test's deliberate-divergence list with their D-row; each is reported, any other difference fails, and a listed line that stops differing fails as stale (D-69: 30 lines). With the JVM's `Math.cos`/`Math.sin` in the Goertzel step 4 OSA values were one ulp off; with fdlibm's (`StrictMath`) none are, and `test/NightMetricsGuardTest.kt` pins one night's series bit for bit. |

Re-derivation: reversed, zero-length and overlapping asserted segments, empty inputs, a trusted archive, edge-touching records, input order, a far-future night. Export coverage: non-finite, negative and zero cadences and minimum gaps, samples on the bounds, a window spanning most of time, an expected count beyond 64 bits. Each guard was proven able to fail by a reverse-edit; the re-derivation's empty-span guard is caught by its hazard test alone. |

The generator now takes about 4.2 s (the order searches). Now 337 cases, 11 292 input and golden lines: 6 061 golden lines, 30 571 doubles, 172 not bit-identical (still only the replayed plans — every new double is bit-identical), 0 mismatches; 189 deliberate-divergence lines (the 184 above and 5 D-89: of the 49 order-sensitive readiness queries, five round one point lower in the generator's fixed dictionary order than in the port's declaration order). E4's sixth slice adds 12 goal-history windows of twelve days (New York, London and Santiago across both 2026 clock changes, Santiago's 6 September without a midnight; spring, fall, all-closed streaks, and skewed windows with duplicated, unsorted and future rows and summaries judged in another zone) → the weekend rule and goal selection, the sleep credit from nights and naps, every built day and the summaries at four instants; and 12 format sweeps (exact binary ties, one-ulp neighbours and decimal fives, NaN, infinities, signed zero, huge values, negative fraction digits and the 510-character cap) → the formatter's six strings per value and width, compared byte for byte; 23 more named branches. Now 361 cases, 12 908 input and golden lines: 7 111 golden lines, 31 299 doubles, 179 not bit-identical (the 172 above and 7 day attainments with three or four rings, which upstream adds in a set's hash order — D-97; every fraction bit-identical), 0 mismatches; every one of the 627 format lines byte-identical; 193 deliberate-divergence lines (the 189 above and 4 D-96: the summaries of Santiago's all-closed 1–12 September, one run of 12 days here where upstream breaks it at 6 September into two runs of 6). E4's seventh slice adds 21 battery cases (clean discharges, charges with dips, resets and unplugging, mixed days with small caps and ages and a missed charge, duplicated times, unsorted histories and stored lists, the guards and floor exactly at and one past their bounds with caps of 0–2, ages of 0, −1, NaN and ±∞ and charge targets outside 0…100, and readings and stored samples outside 0…100) → both folds and both estimates after every reading, the final histories, and per stored list the time to empty, the depletion date (with Foundation's reference date as `now`, so the date compares exactly) and the time to full at every target, and the "full" queries; 31 more named branches. A coverage check found one slice-2 branch named in the generator but never reached (a resting HR from the lowest sustained window on a day with no usable sleep); 4 days of a new no-night shape reach it, appended without changing any earlier case. Every named branch is hit at least once. Final: 386 cases, 13 996 input and golden lines; the generator runs in about 4.3 s; a second run is byte-identical; 7 879 golden lines, 33 723 doubles, 195 not bit-identical (the replayed plans' summation order, now 188 with the four new days, and the 7 attainments), none in a battery line, 0 mismatches; 230 deliberate-divergence lines (the 193 above and 37 D-99: the lines of the three hostile battery cases that a percent outside 0…100 reaches). |

It runs upstream's own engine functions at the pin at named sites only — where a line-for-line port can silently differ (libm calls, summation order, rounding, date arithmetic) — over seeded synthetic inputs. First sites, the trends engine: 36 day lists in 9 shapes (typical, sparse, guard edges, unreadable values and 32-bit counts, short, long, stepped, exactly at the 3 % and 10 % thresholds, zero priors) → `rollingAverages` at six windows and `trend` at five windows × 6 extractors × 3 minimum changes; 24 bedtime lists in 6 shapes (regular, irregular, around midnight, outside 0…1439 and at the 32-bit ends, opposite, short) → `sleepRegularity` at six windows; every constant bedtime list of 2, 3 and 7 nights; and the platform's `cos` and `sin` at all 1 440 bedtime angles and its `log` at 240 values, compared with `StrictMath`. Files: `ringkit/src/test/resources/engine-differential/` (`inputs.txt`, `goldens.txt`, `coverage.txt`; the format is documented at the top of the generator). The test compares whole lines, discrete values exactly and doubles within 1e-9, lists every double that is not bit-identical, keeps a deliberate-divergence list that fails on an unlisted and on a stale difference, and reads its branch list from the generator's own code (comments stripped; every branch named by a string literal, a non-literal name fails the reader), so a branch added there and never reached fails without anyone updating a list. First run: 66 cases, 1 299 input and golden lines; the generator runs in 34 ms and a second run is byte-identical; the sleep and vitals goldens unchanged; 27 named branches, all reached; 513 golden lines, 6 150 doubles, 154 not bit-identical (each a platform libm value exactly one ulp from `StrictMath` — 128 of the sweep's 2 880 cos and sin values, 14 in the bedtime lists' own, 12 of the 240 logs; none in an engine output), 0 mismatches; 30 deliberate-divergence lines, every line of the three constant-bedtime cases (D-107). Second sites (E5a's second slice), the headache signals index and its evaluation, appended (the trends cases byte-identical): 40 days in 10 shapes (typical, deviant, sparse, cold start, ring silence, truncated with fever / already-logged, finite extremes, unreadable readings, banded, capped) → `HeadacheSignals.assess` — the verdict, every contribution and the quotient the index is rounded from; 3 sweeps of one series' today over 512 steps → the index's rounding at and around every half; `percentile` over 12 sorted lists; `midranks`, `auc` and `hanleyMcNeilSE` over 16 value lists (ties, signed zeros, NaN, past Swift's insertion-sort size) and 4 grids; the exact hypergeometric tail over 16 sets of tables up to 100 000 rows and every guard — each value with whether it clears the 0.01 working alpha, a discrete answer a last-bit difference in `log` or `exp` could flip — and the Wilson bound. The generator also writes `random.txt`: the Swift standard library's seeded random draws the headache evaluation tests are built from (read by `test/SwiftRandomTest.kt`, row below). Second run: 157 cases, 2 990 input, golden and random-draw lines (bound 6 000); generator 134 ms; byte-identical on a second run; 64 named branches, all reached; 761 golden lines, 9 537 doubles, 183 not bit-identical (the 154 above and 29 exact-tail values, one ulp each, none flipping the alpha), 0 mismatches; 42 deliberate-divergence lines: the 30 above and the verdict, contributions and quotient of the four days holding an unreadable reading (D-108). Third sites (E5a's fifth slice), cycle prediction, appended (the earlier cases byte-identical): 28 period histories in 7 shapes (whole-day, fractional-second, stale — rolled forward 100 and 1 000 years and to Foundation's distant future — across both 2026 New York clock changes with New York named in the generator, hostile, skin-temperature nights at and around the corroboration window's edges, and the length, duration and roll-forward edges) → `cycleStats` and `predict` at 3 or 4 clocks each; every date is written as Foundation holds it, a double of seconds since 2001, and a predicted date is compared within 1e-6 s. Third run: 185 cases, 3 387 input, golden and random-draw lines; generator 154 ms; byte-identical on a second run; 77 named branches, all reached; 893 golden lines, 9 977 doubles, 183 not bit-identical (all of them the earlier ones: every cycle value is bit-identical), 0 mismatches; 42 deliberate-divergence lines (unchanged). The same maths in plain `Instant` nanoseconds misses 14 lines by more than 1e-6 s (D-119). Fourth sites (E5a's last slice), ring proximity, appended (the earlier cases byte-identical): every link reading from −110 to +10 dBm and the sentinels and 32-bit ends (no reading, −1 000, −101, −100, −99, −96, −95, −59, −45, −1, 0, 1, 127) → `approximateMeters` (`pow`), `approximateFeet` and `distanceText` (written as its UTF-8 bytes). Final run: 188 cases; 3 408 input, golden and random-draw lines without the file headers (3 411 with them, the count the third run quoted); generator 159 ms, script 22 s; byte-identical on a second run; 82 named branches, all reached; 902 golden lines, 10 187 doubles, 201 not bit-identical (the 183 above and 18 distances, 9 in metres and 9 in feet, one ulp from `StrictMath.pow`, largest 1.4e-14), 0 mismatches — every distance text identical, so no last-bit difference crosses a text edge; 42 deliberate-divergence lines (unchanged); the Kotlin class 0.15 s. |

Android reads the ring's MAC straight from `BluetoothDevice`, so `RingAuth` always computes the real response. | The 24 captured challenge→response pairs become test fixtures for `RingAuth` (E1 slice 2). `CommandTableTest` and `FrameTest` check that the members stay absent. |
| D-2 | **DEVIATION: out-of-range bytes rejected.** Kotlin `Command` builders and `RingAuth.response` / `RingAuth.authCommand` (the `challenge` parameter) take `Int` where Swift took `UInt8`. They throw `IllegalArgumentException` for a value outside 0–255 instead of truncating it. | Swift's type made an out-of-range byte impossible; silently truncating `0x100` to `0x00` would send the wrong command. | — (done) |
| D-3 | **DEVIATION: `Command.syncSince` never overflows.** Swift's `UInt32(clamping: unixSeconds - syncEpoch)` traps if the subtraction overflows. Kotlin compares against the epoch before subtracting, so any `Long` input clamps to `00000000`…`ffffffff`. | A plain Kotlin subtraction would wrap near `Long.MIN_VALUE` and produce a far-future cursor. Results are identical to upstream for every input upstream accepts. | — (done) |

| D-12 | **DEVIATION: `FirmwareInfo` is immutable.** Its fields are `val`, not `var`. The ported tests build it with `FirmwareInfo(version = …)` instead of mutating it. | Upstream's `var` fields sit on a Swift struct, which copies on assignment. A Kotlin class is a shared reference, so a `var` would let one holder change another's snapshot, and an in-place change never emits through a `StateFlow`. `copy(...)` keeps upstream's value semantics. Found by the Phase 3.5 reviewer fan-out. | — (done). E7 (`:ble`) accumulates DIS reads with `copy(...)`. |
| D-13 | **DEVIATION: Swift's value-type guarantees are spelled out.** `Frame.Parsed` throws `IllegalArgumentException` for an `opcode` or `trailer` outside 0–255, copies `body` on construction and returns a fresh copy on every read. `SportFrame.Sample` throws for a `cursor` outside 0–0xFFFFFFFF. | Swift's `UInt8` / `UInt32` made those values impossible, and its `[UInt8]` copies on assignment. A shared, writable Kotlin `ByteArray` let one holder change another's frame and move its `hashCode` (the same class as D-12). Every value upstream can produce is accepted unchanged. Reviewed and deliberately NOT changed, because each matches upstream and a check would reject input upstream accepts: `QuantitySample` / `SleepSegment` accept `end` before `start` and a NaN `value` (a throwing constructor could drop a segment, against D-10); `SkinTemperature`, `CaseBattery` and `Sample.hr`/`steps` carry no range check (their decoders already enforce the bands); `DeviceStatus` stays functions over a raw frame; `LiveHR.settled` does not re-filter (pinned by the ported settling tests); `DecodeAnomaly.hasSustainedTemperatureAnomaly` treats NaN as in-band and does not validate its parameters (documented in its KDoc; its only producer cannot emit NaN). Found by the Phase 5.5 reviewer fan-out. | — (done; `test/PortedTypeGuardTest.kt`) |
| D-14 | **DEVIATION: `SyncCursor` and `EpochSyncSession` are classes with `copy()`.** Upstream both are Swift `mutating struct`s, so every assignment was an independent copy. Here each is a mutable class owned by one drain: sharing the reference shares the state, and `copy()` gives a fully independent snapshot (a session's buffered pages are copied too). `SyncCursor.selectNewStaged` returns a `Staged(fresh, advanced)` data class instead of a tuple; it destructures the same way. The ported `advancedKinds` test writes `base.copy()` where upstream writes `var moved = base`. | The watermark and the page buffer change in place on every page of a drain. Keeping them mutable keeps upstream's call shape; `copy()` keeps its value semantics wherever a caller needs a snapshot, and `selectNewStaged` still never moves the cursor it is called on. | — (done; `test/HistoryTypeGuardTest.kt`) |
| D-15 | **DEVIATION: `CumulativeMetricState` is immutable.** Its fields are `val`, not `var`; derive the next state with `copy(...)`. | The same reason as D-12: upstream's `var` fields sit on a Swift struct, which copies on assignment, while a Kotlin class is a shared reference. | — (done; `test/HistoryTypeGuardTest.kt`) |

Every value upstream can produce is accepted unchanged. | — (done; `test/HistoryTypeGuardTest.kt`) |

Byte values are `Int`s and enum cases are upper-snake throughout this port. Behaviour is unchanged. | — (done) |

| D-68 | **DEVIATION (owner decision): this morning's wake in the days after a spring-forward clock change.** Before the nearest sleep window's bedtime, upstream's `MissedNight.morningWake` steps `now` back a fixed 24 hours and takes the nearest window again. Kotlin steps back one calendar day from the nearest window's wake and places the wake on that day by the same rule as every other wake (the day's start plus the schedule's minutes). It does not step `now` itself back a calendar day: on a fall-back evening that is 25 hours and would land on yesterday's wake. The two rules differ only in the 56 hours after a change that loses time, in two ways. (1) A stale wake: on the evening of the changed day, and again on the evening of the day after, 24 hours back lands where an older wake is the nearer one, so upstream answers yesterday's wake (measured at 10-minute resolution for a 22:30–06:30 schedule: New York 2026-03-08 19:10–19:30 and 2026-03-09 18:40–19:00, London 2026-03-29 and 2026-03-30 and St John's 2026-03-08 and 2026-03-09 likewise, Chatham 2026-09-27 19:05–19:25 and 2026-09-28 18:35–18:55, Lord Howe 2026-10-04 18:50–19:00 and 2026-10-05 18:40; Santiago, Havana and Beirut on the day after only, 18:40–19:30). In that window a sync made yesterday evening counts as "after this morning's wake", so the missed-night banner can read MISSING where "not synced yet" was meant. (2) A wake an hour late: where the lost hour is the one after midnight (Santiago, Havana, Beirut), the changed day starts at 01:00 and upstream builds the next day's wake from that start, so on the evening of the day after the change (19:40–22:20 for the same schedule) it answers 07:30 for a 06:30 wake, although at noon the same day it answered 06:30; a sync made at 07:00 is then after the wake at noon and before it in the evening. Kotlin answers the scheduled wake all day. Sweep against upstream's rule, every 10 minutes across ±72 h of every 2026 transition in eleven zones with ten schedules: 816 of 190 300 answers change — 369 where upstream's was stale (Kotlin's is later), 447 where upstream's was an hour late (Kotlin's is earlier; the three midnight-gap zones only). Every changed answer is a wake the sleep window itself gives and has already passed; no fall-back or fixed-offset point changes. Measured over the whole of 2026 (5 781 600 points): the same 816 and nothing else. At one-minute resolution: 8 343 of 1 901 020. The owner chose the calendar-day step over keeping upstream's answer; the first measurement behind that choice covered only ±30 h (109 of 79 420, the changed day's evening alone), and the wider sweep replaced it before this port was merged. | The banner's wording on those evenings; a short, rare window, but the wrong one tells the user a night is missing, or changes its mind during the day. | — (done; `test/MissedNightHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-69 | **DEVIATION (owner decision): night selection stops at a declined bridge.** In `latestNightRecords`' backward cluster chain, upstream skips a bridge the observed-gap guard declines (`continue`) and keeps walking back. So a block that lies behind an UNOBSERVED hole further back can still be absorbed past the declined one, and the window then spans the declined block too, pulling its records back into the night — the very records the guard had just ruled out (measured on the pinned build: a 4 h night with a short block behind a fully observed awake hour and another short block behind an empty 2 h hole returns 180 records from the earliest block's start, identical to the guard switched off). Upstream's own comment calls this case untested and `break` "arguably cleaner". Kotlin ends the chain at the first declined bridge: that archive returns the 4 h night alone, 106 records. A bridge that is not declined chains exactly as before, and with the guard off nothing changes. Every ported upstream selection vector passes unchanged; in the Swift differential the 30 `sel` lines of the 10 `leapfrog` archives (UTC; shipped defaults, re-anchor off, morning absorb off) move, and no other line does (listed in `SleepDifferentialTest`'s deliberate-divergence list). The owner chose this over keeping upstream's walk. | A guard that declines a bridge should not be undone by the same walk one step later; the night it returns is the one the guard meant. | — (done; `test/NightSelectionHazardTest.kt`, `test/SleepDifferentialTest.kt`) |
| D-70 | **DEVIATION (owner decision): a duplicated record counts once in sleep detection, staging, night selection and naps.** Upstream reads every copy of a record. The detector's stillness window counts samples, so with every record twice the main block widened by 90 s at each end (measured: 1781351506..1781383696 against the single night's 1781351596..1781383606) and a nap likewise (1781547690..1781551080 against 1781547780..1781550990); staging staged the doubled timeline (6 segments over a wider in-bed span); `latestNightRecords` returned both copies (144 of 72); and `bridgeIsDeclined` counted a time once per copy, so a half-observed awake hour with every record doubled read as complete and was declined. Kotlin reads each counter once, keeping its FIRST copy in the caller's order and the input order otherwise, as a new list (the caller's is never changed): one helper, `BulkSleep.distinctRecords`, at the four record entries — `BulkSleep.mainSleep`, `BulkSleep.sleepSegments`, `BulkSleep.latestNightRecords` and `SleepStaging.classify` (so `stagedSegments` too) — plus `NapDetection.naps`; and `bridgeIsDeclined`, which takes sample times, counts each distinct time once. A doubled night, staging, selection and nap are exactly the single ones. Staging and naps are included because they read the block the detector finds: with detection alone deduplicated, a doubled night staged as neither upstream's nor the single night (measured), a mix. Metrics that take records directly (movement, stress, per-stage HR, averages, samples) are unchanged. `EpochArchive.merge` already deduplicates by counter before any of this; this is the same rule one layer down. No ported upstream vector changes, and no Swift-differential line moves (its inputs hold no duplicated counter). The owner chose this over keeping upstream's reading. | A record delivered twice (a re-drained page, a caller that skipped the archive merge) must not move a night's edges, change its stages or make an awake hour look observed. | — (done; `test/SleepDetectionHazardTest.kt`, `test/NightSelectionHazardTest.kt`, `test/SleepStagingHazardTest.kt`, `test/NapDetectionHazardTest.kt`) |

| D-73 | **DEFERRED: range checks on energy inputs and outputs.** The energy functions return what upstream's arithmetic gives for an impossible input, as upstream does (measured): a profile weight of −70 kg gives −70 kcal for 2 km and a BMR of 280; an all-zero female profile gives a BMR of −161; a NaN weight gives NaN energy (Keytel excepted, where Swift's `max(0, NaN)` is 0); an infinite distance or workout duration gives infinite energy, or NaN with a zero Keytel rate; one heart-rate sample spanning ±3e16 s gives 1.5e16 kcal of TRIMP energy. Nothing here crashes, loops or allocates, and `:ringkit` writes nothing. Also kept as upstream: duplicated heart-rate samples each count in TRIMP energy (the archive merge removes duplicates by counter before samples are built — `test/VitalsTracerTest.kt`); the stress index of an empty, single or constant RR list is 10.0; strain from a non-finite sample interval (NaN gives 0.0; +∞ gives ∞, or 0.0 with a zone-0 reading). | A clamp here could not repair NaN and would still pass 1.5e16. The right bounds belong to the consumers: the profile's valid ranges where it is entered and stored, the daily estimate's and the write ledger's own checks, and Health Connect's documented record ranges where energy is written. | E23 (profile entry and the stored profile); E10 (the Health Connect writer, against each record type's documented range); the energy ledger (E4's third slice). E4's second slice kept the daily estimate as upstream computes it (a NaN weight gives a NaN total) and bounded only its bucket width (D-74). E4's third slice: the write ledger plans nothing from NaN, infinite or negative bucket energy (D-79); finite energy, however large, is planned as computed, so the writer's range check (E10) remains. |

Upstream walks every bucket of the span: measured on the pinned build, a 1 ms width made 1.8 million buckets in 2.3 s for one 30-minute step window, 1 µs ran past a 20 s kill, 1e-300 s traps on the `Int` conversion, and an infinite width gives a bucket that starts at NaN (1e300 s, one that ends past any date an `Instant` holds). The only valid-looking input that changes is a width between 0 and 1 s, which upstream attributes in sub-second buckets; production uses 900 s. The vitals differential lists the 8 lines this moves (widths 0.5 s and 2e9 s on its hostile days). | A caller constant must not be able to hang the app, exhaust its memory, crash it or write a NaN timestamp; a one-second grid is finer than any Health Connect bucket. | — (done; `test/DailyEnergyHazardTest.kt`, with a time limit that fails rather than hangs) |
| D-75 | **DEVIATION (bound): instants saturate at the ends of `Instant`'s range.** A widened point sample in `ExerciseMinutes` whose end would pass the last instant `java.time` holds (an `epochSeconds` or `pointSampleWidth` of +∞ or 1e300 s, or a sample within 150 s of `Instant.MAX`) ends at `Instant.MAX`, where upstream's `Date` reaches infinity (measured: ∞ and 1.67e298 minutes). Window tests in `RestingHR` and `ExerciseMinutes` compare exact second differences instead of adding to an instant, so nothing overflows. A heart-rate reading no zone can place in a calendar day (the last year of `Instant`'s range) is left out of `RestingHR.dailyValues`; a reading 31.7 million years out gets its real start of day, where Foundation's calendar answers a day 500 000 years earlier (measured: a 1e15 s reading keyed to 1.59e13 s). Every instant a ring or a phone can produce is unaffected. | `Instant` cannot represent infinity; a corrupt stored timestamp must not throw out of an analytics function. | — (done; `test/ExerciseMinutesHazardTest.kt`, `test/RestingHRHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-76 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the resting-HR, exercise-minute and daily-estimate port.** (1) `RestingHR.dailyValues` takes a required `ZoneId` (upstream defaults to the device calendar): days start at `CalendarDay.startOfDay` and the next day is one calendar day later in wall-clock time, as Foundation's `date(byAdding: .day)` (Santiago's 6 September 2026, which has no midnight, runs 01:00 → 01:00). (2) Upstream's `TimeInterval` parameters (`window`, `epochSeconds`, `pointSampleWidth`, `bucketSeconds`) are `Double` seconds, accepting what upstream accepts. (3) Counts stay `Int`, upstream's 64-bit `Int` arithmetic is kept where it matters: credited steps, the residual and the qualifying-bpm sum are `Long` (a 32-bit sum wraps on three `Int.MAX_VALUE` step windows or two `Int.MAX_VALUE` readings); the max HR `220 − age` is computed in 64 bits and saturated to `Int.MAX_VALUE` — upstream's answer changes only for an age below −2 147 483 427, where both thresholds lie above every real heart rate (measured: the same estimate). (4) `RestingHR.DailyValue`, `Calories.EnergyBucket` and `Calories.DailyEstimate` compare doubles by IEEE `==`, as Swift's synthesized `Equatable` (NaN unequal to itself, −0.0 equal to 0.0); `DailyEstimate.buckets` is copied in and read-only out; `StepWindow` and `ElevatedPiece` are data classes. (5) `lowestSustainedDetailed` returns a small value type in place of Swift's tuple. Also kept as upstream after measuring: a duplicated isolated spot read earns a full exercise epoch, and a day of duplicated spot reads passes the switched-off personalised baseline's spot-read guard (removing duplicates is the record merge's job, as D-73); a sustained window of 0, below 0 or NaN on a single reading gives a NaN resting HR (a caller constant; production uses 300 s); a sleep floor of 0 or below gives 0.0 when no reading falls in sleep; a step window that ends before it starts is credited whole at its start, a negative delta is skipped and steps below zero earn nothing. | Upstream's numbers for every input the API accepts, with no default calendar; one home per type. | — (done; `test/DailyEnergyGuardTest.kt`, `test/RestingHRHazardTest.kt`, `test/ExerciseMinutesHazardTest.kt`, `test/DailyEnergyHazardTest.kt`) |

| D-78 | **DEVIATION (bound): the energy write ledger's grid is the daily estimate's.** `ActiveEnergyLedger.plan` and `seed` accept bucket widths from 1 s to 1e9 s (the widths `Calories.dailyEstimate` attributes with, D-74) and slots 0 to ⌊26 h / width⌋ after the day start (the estimate's attribution span). Outside the width range `plan` writes nothing and returns the state it was given, and `seed` carries the whole legacy total as debt — upstream's own answer for a width of 0 or below. A bucket that starts more than 26 h after the day start is skipped, exactly as upstream skips one that starts before it. Upstream sizes its watermark array by any ordinal: measured on the pinned build, a 1 ms width puts a 01:00 bucket in slot 3.6 million, a 1e-300 s width traps on the `Int` conversion (in `ordinal` and `seed`), a bucket ten years out builds 350 401 marks, and one at 3e16 s fails to allocate 2.7e14 bytes; 2e9 s and ∞ fold the whole day into slot 0. `ordinal` itself keeps upstream's arithmetic, returns 64 bits and saturates where Swift traps. | D-79 | **DEVIATION: the energy write ledger fails closed on a value it cannot read.** `ActiveEnergyLedger.plan` writes nothing and returns the state it was given when a bucket of the day has NaN, infinite or negative energy, the day's total is not finite, a stored watermark is NaN, infinite or negative, or the carry, the workout credit or the saved total is NaN or infinite. A negative carry, credit or saved total still counts as zero, as upstream (a credit goes negative when a workout is deleted). Upstream, measured: a watermark of −40 writes 65 kcal into a 40 kcal bucket (only the day-total clamp, and only with a correct saved total, stops more); −∞ writes 65 kcal and stores NaN; a NaN watermark strands its bucket and stays NaN; a NaN carry, credit or saved total counts as 0 and the whole day is written again — the debt or the saved total that could not be read is forgotten; a +∞ bucket plans an infinite write; −10 kcal in one bucket cuts another bucket's write; two 1e308 buckets plan an infinite day. The state a plan hands back from readable input is always readable, so the rule never stops a valid day (a sweep of 300 seeded days, 12 committed flushes each). The vitals differential lists the 20 lines this moves (five unreadable states on each of its hostile days). | The health store sums active energy, so an over-write is permanent; one corrupt record stopping that day's writes is an under-write the next day heals. | — (done; `test/ActiveEnergyLedgerHazardTest.kt`, `test/VitalsDifferentialTest.kt`) |
| D-80 | **DEVIATION: upgrade-day seeding never re-pays energy.** `ActiveEnergyLedger.seed` adds the fills of buckets that share a slot, where upstream overwrites the slot with the last one while still taking every fill from the legacy total (measured: two 40 kcal buckets on one slot with 80 kcal already written seed a mark of 40, and the next plan writes those 40 kcal again). A NaN legacy total is read as covering the day — upstream's own answer for an infinite one: every bucket full and infinite debt, so nothing more is written that day — where upstream reads it as 0 and the next plan writes the whole day again (measured: 65 kcal). When a bucket of the day has NaN, infinite or negative energy, nothing is placed and the whole legacy total is carried as debt (upstream: a NaN mark and NaN carry; a mark of −10 that grows the carry). (2) `Write`, `Plan` and `Seed` are immutable classes comparing doubles by IEEE `==`, as Swift's synthesized `Equatable` (NaN unequal to itself, −0.0 equal to 0.0); `Plan.writes`, `Plan.watermarks` and `Seed.watermarks` are copied in and read-only out; `Seed` replaces upstream's `(watermarks:, carry:)` tuple and destructures the same way. (3) Widths stay `Double` seconds; `resolve` returns the shared `DateInterval`; `widenedStart` stays internal. (4) Instants are exact to the nanosecond where a Swift `Date` carries a double (the two agree on a millisecond grid), and a window that would widen past the start of `Instant`'s range saturates there. Kept as upstream after measuring: a stored watermark list longer than the day nets its positive tail into debt and keeps its length, a shorter one is extended with zeros; buckets sharing a slot add up in `plan`; unsorted buckets are sorted; a bucket that ends before it starts, or has not begun at `now`, stays owed; a NaN minimum write never writes; NaN, negative or subnormal energy does not widen a window, and ∞ or huge energy starts it at the day start; an anchor at `now` widens back over time the last window covered (placement only — the amount comes from the ledger). | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; one home per type. | — (done; `test/EnergyLedgerGuardTest.kt`, `test/ActiveEnergyLedgerHazardTest.kt`, `test/ActiveEnergyWindowHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-82 | **DEVIATION: the energy ledger sums the day's total in ascending bucket order.** `ActiveEnergyLedger.plan` adds the day's per-bucket energy in ascending ordinal, where upstream adds a dictionary's values (`ActiveEnergyLedger.swift:169`) in an order Swift seeds per process — so upstream's own last bit is not fixed from one run to the next (three runs of the differential generator gave three different golden files). The total only bounds the writes (the day-total backstop), so a plan can differ from upstream's in the last bit of a clamped write: 172 of 27 804 differential doubles, largest difference 3.4e-13; with upstream's sum taken in ascending ordinal (a probe build) every double matches bit for bit. | D-83 | **DEVIATION: in the baselines an unreadable (NaN or infinite) reading is a missing reading.** Upstream counts it. In the port: unreadable prior days and nights are dropped before the trailing window (fewer than the minimum readable → no baseline); an unreadable today is not judged (`classify` gives upstream's own no-baseline answer: normal, no baseline, delta 0); an unreadable offset or resting HR is absent (`suspectedFever` false, `report` no temperature signal); readings with an unreadable temperature are dropped before the count floor and the coverage (`coverage` itself reads only times); `anomalyFlags` raises nothing for an unreadable tonight and treats an unreadable baseline or previous night as absent; `report` keeps the baseline but gives an unreadable tonight no offset, no band and no flags; `VitalsBaseline.tempSeverity` and `SkinTempBaseline.deviationBand` return null for a NaN offset (an infinite one keeps its band). Finite input takes exactly upstream's path: inserting unreadable values anywhere into 500 seeded finite series leaves the stats bit-identical. | A stored day or night that cannot be read is not a day judged normal, and an infinity is not an alarm. | — (done; `test/RobustBaselineHazardTest.kt`, `test/VitalsBaselineHazardTest.kt`, `test/SkinTempBaselineHazardTest.kt`, `test/VitalsDifferentialTest.kt` — 104 listed lines) |
| D-84 | **DEVIATION (bound): a baseline window of zero or negative days or nights has no baseline.** Upstream traps on a negative window — `VitalsBaseline.stats` with a negative `maxBaselineDays`, `SkinTempBaseline.baseline` and `report` with a negative `windowNights` ("Can't take a suffix of negative length from a collection", measured) — and averages an empty window with a minimum of 0 or below into NaN (`VitalsBaseline` mean NaN with n 0; `SkinTempBaseline.baseline` NaN). Over finite days from −1e308 to 1e308, four MADs and four floors, z stays inside the clamp and never falls as today rises. Callers that pass a floor of 0.3 or more cannot reach the difference. | D-90 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the scores, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `WellnessBalance.Input` and `ActivityScore.Input` — upstream structs with `var` fields — are immutable data classes changed with `copy`; `ActivityScore.Input` compares its doubles by IEEE `==`, as upstream's `Equatable` struct (−0.0 equals 0.0, NaN is unequal to itself), and `WellnessBalance.Input` holds no doubles. | D-91 | **DEVIATION: goal settings are read through a caller-supplied lookup, with Foundation's number bridging; an integer beyond 32 bits reads as missing.** Upstream reads `UserDefaults.standard` with `object(forKey:) as? Int` / `as? Double`. `:ringkit` has no settings store, so `GoalDefaults.stepsGoal` / `sleepGoalMinutes` and `GoalHistory.Goals.fromDefaults` take a `GoalSettings` (`fun interface GoalSettings { fun value(key: String): Any? }`) the app supplies over its own store. Measured on the pinned build with a real suite (36 stored values), `as? Int` takes any number whose value is whole (9000.0 → 9000, −0.0 → 0, a float 9000 → 9000, `true` → 1, `false` → 0) and `as? Double` any number the double holds exactly (an integer → x.0, the float 0.1 → 0.10000000149011612, NaN, ∞); a fractional value read as an integer, NaN or ∞ read as an integer, 2^53 + 1 read as a double, a string (even "9000"), a list, a map, a date or data read as missing, and the default answers. The port applies that rule to Kotlin's boxed `Int`, `Long`, `Short`, `Byte`, `Double`, `Float` and `Boolean`; anything else is the wrong type. Bound: upstream's integers are 64-bit, so a stored 2^31 or 2^40 is returned as the goal; a Kotlin goal is an `Int`, and a value it cannot hold reads as missing (the default) rather than a wrapped or saturated goal nobody set. A lookup that throws is not caught. | No ambient settings store; every value in the `Int` range reads exactly as iOS reads it. | — (done; `test/GoalDefaultsHazardTest.kt`, `test/GoalUnitGuardTest.kt`) |
| D-92 | **DEVIATION: default units come from the locale passed in — US and Liberia get °F and miles; the locale's unit preference and region override count; a locale with no region is metric.** Upstream reads `Locale.current.measurementSystem == .us`. Measured on this Mac with the pinned build's Foundation across all 292 regions: only US and LR use the US system — Myanmar uses the UK system, so it gets °C and km (an older list of US-system regions included Myanmar; the port follows the measurement). Upstream also honours the locale's own unit preference (`-u-ms-metric` / `ussystem` / `uksystem`) before a region override (`-u-rg-xxzzzz`) before the region, and so does the port. The JDK has no measurement-system data, so the rule is that two-region table plus the two keywords. A locale with no region (`en`, the root locale) reads metric: Foundation fills a region in from the language (`en` → US), and `java.util.Locale` has no such table; a device locale carries a region. | No ambient locale; the same answer as iOS for every locale with a region. | — (done; `test/LocaleDefaultUnitsTest.kt`, `test/UnitsFormatterHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-93 | **DEVIATION (bound): one shared fixed-decimal formatter reproduces Foundation's `%.Nf` and never crashes.** The three `UnitsFormatter` number shapes (`UnitPreferences.swift:99`, `:108`, `:115`) and the archive diagnostics' gap hours use `swiftFixed` in `SwiftNumerics.kt`. Measured on the pinned build: the exact binary value is rounded ties to even (0.25 → "0.2", 0.125 → "0.12", 2.5 → "2"; 0.35 → "0.3", as it sits below its tie); NaN prints "nan" whatever its sign and even when a sign is forced; infinities print "inf", "+inf", "-inf"; −0.0, and a negative value that rounds to zero, print "-0.0"; large values print their whole decimal expansion; a negative `fractionDigits` builds "%.-Nf", which Foundation reads as no decimals left-justified in a field N wide; the number is cut to its first 510 characters; and once \|`fractionDigits`\| reaches 2^31 − 512 upstream crashes with a segmentation fault. The port writes the same text for every `Int` width, computing at most 1 100 decimals (a double's expansion has at most 1 074, and only 510 characters are kept), so a huge width allocates nothing and the crashing widths get the capped text. Java's `String.format` is not used: it rounds half up and reads the default locale. The diagnostics' `oneDecimal` now delegates; its text is unchanged for every value the report can print (non-negative gap hours — swept over 3.1 million values), and differs only for −0.0, negatives that round to zero and non-finite values, now as upstream's `%.1f`. | Byte-identical strings for every width upstream answers; no crash where upstream crashes; one home for the rule. | — (done; `test/UnitsFormatterHazardTest.kt`, `test/VitalsDifferentialTest.kt` — 627 format lines byte-identical) |

(2) No ambient zone: `withinDeliveryWindow`, `dayKey` and `candidates` take a required `ZoneId` where upstream defaults `Calendar.current`; `candidates` keeps upstream's defaulted `tuning`, `topSignals` its `limit` of 2. `dayKey` and `freshForDay` forward to the temperature routing's (one implementation each), so the key is a `Long?` and the per-day ledger a `Map<HealthNotification, Long>` (Swift's 64-bit `Int`). (4) Kept differences, measured on the pinned build: an instant `java.time` cannot place in the zone (the first and last year of `Instant`'s range) is outside the delivery window and has no day key, so nothing is raised on a clock the port cannot read; before about 4713 BC Foundation's time of day stops being the wall clock (over 400 000 random instants it agrees from then on; at −1e13 s upstream answers inside the window, here 06:13:20, outside). (5) Kept as upstream after measuring: a NaN, zero, negative or −∞ share is never named, +∞ shares tie, a limit below zero names none; frozen-day counts and unlock floors at the `Int` ends compare as plain numbers; the per-day ledger keeps duplicates and order; the calendar lookup (`perimenstrual`) is worded when passed to `copy` (`topSignals` never passes it); a third signal passed to `copy` is ignored. (6) Test side: upstream's `Character.isNumber` is reproduced by `test/SwiftCharacter.kt` (a number category or one of 99 numeric ideographs), equal to Swift's on JDK 17 for every scalar but 143 assigned after Unicode 13. | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; the zone is the caller's and the copy never depends on the machine. | — (done; `test/HealthAlertsHeadacheTest.kt`, `test/HealthAlertsGuardTest.kt`, `test/HealthAlertsHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-116 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the reminders, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `ReminderKind` keeps upstream's three raw names in declaration order, equal to the `HealthNotification` reminder cases' (one de-dupe namespace), with `fromRawValue` an exact match. `SedentaryReminder` and `WearReminder` are immutable and change through `copy`, comparing their doubles by IEEE `==` as Swift's synthesized `Equatable`; `BedtimeReminder` is a data class. The predicates are pure: the app routes their survivors through the existing `NotificationGate` as the matching `HealthNotification` case. (2) No ambient zone or clock: `SedentaryReminder.shouldFire` and `BedtimeReminder.shouldFire` take a required `ZoneId` where upstream defaults `Calendar.current`; every `now` is required. `TimeInterval`s are `Double` seconds; the gap is `secondsBetween`, an exact `Instant` difference, so a stamp 1 ns short of the interval is short here, where upstream's `Date` doubles near 2026 read it as the whole interval (measured; 1 µs short agrees). (3) Swift's 64-bit `Int`: the bedtime window's start is computed in 64 bits with Swift's truncating remainder, so stored minutes at the `Int` ends answer as upstream (measured) and no pair wraps (a 32-bit expression reads `Int.MAX − Int.MIN` as −1); upstream traps only past 64 bits. (4) Kept difference, measured on the pinned build: an instant `java.time` cannot place in the zone (the first and last year of `Instant`'s range) raises no sedentary or bedtime reminder and nothing throws, where Foundation still reads a time of day there; far instants it can place (to 1e13 s, back to Foundation's distant past) agree with upstream's time of day. (5) Kept as upstream after measuring: the stamps that suppress (off-finger, last frame, worn evidence, the charger's last frame) are clamped at 0, so a future one is "just now"; the stamps that make a reminder due (last activity; the wear rule's last frame) are not, so a future one is "not yet" — clamping those too would change nothing for any positive interval (swept); NaN, infinite, zero and negative intervals and graces; an inverted sedentary window is empty (it does not wrap) and one wider than the day always open; bedtimes and leads outside the day compare as plain numbers (a lead of 0 or a whole day is an empty window, bed == wake never fires); both 2026 clock changes read the zone's wall clock (a window inside the skipped hour never opens that night). | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; the zone and the clock are the caller's. | — (done; `test/ReminderGuardTest.kt`, `test/ReminderEngineHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-117 | **DEVIATION (bound): the cycle predictor's roll-forward is bounded.** `predict` adds the average cycle to the predicted start one addition at a time, as upstream does, for up to 2²⁰ cycles — enough for every date Foundation represents (its distant future from 2026 is about 35 000 additions, measured; 1e12 s is 412 637) — and past that adds the remaining whole cycles in one step and finishes with upstream's loop. Upstream's loop always ends (the average is 21…45 days), but at `Instant`'s ends it would add about 1.3e10 times, for minutes. Past the bound the answer is still the first step at or after `now`; a step past `Instant`'s end saturates at `Instant.MAX`. | A clock no phone shows must not hang a prediction; every clock upstream answers in practice takes upstream's exact path. | — (done; `test/CyclePredictorHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-118 | **DEVIATION (improvement): the period mirror counts calendar days when a period starts on a day whose midnight the zone skips.** Where a clock change skips midnight itself (Santiago, Havana and Beirut in 2026), that day starts at 01:00. Upstream adds days to that 01:00 and counts whole days from it, so a period starting that day is mirrored one day short — measured on days 0 / 6 / 7 / 8 / 12: an open period 1, 6, 7, 8, 8 days (the cap reached a day late), with 10 days already covered 1, 6, 7, 8, 10, and a 5-day logged period 4 days for good. The port counts calendar days in the zone: 1, 7, 8, 8, 8 / 1, 7, 8, 9, 10 / 5, and a last day is the start of its calendar day. Every other start agrees with upstream, including starts whose span crosses the skipped midnight. | One day of a period the wearer logged never reached the health store; the cap and the anti-retraction floor keep their meaning. | — (done; `test/CyclePredictorHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-119 | **DEVIATION: the cycle predictor keeps Foundation's `Double`-seconds date arithmetic and converts to `Instant` once.** Upstream's `Date` is one `Double` of seconds since 2001, and `cycleStats` and `predict` subtract, add, compare and roll forward in those doubles, rounding at every step. The port reads each `Instant` as that double (whole seconds since 2001 plus nanoseconds / 1e9), does upstream's arithmetic, and converts each result to an `Instant` once, to the nearest nanosecond; at today's dates a double holds about a tenth of a microsecond, so the round trip loses nothing. Measured with the engine differential (28 histories, 440 values): every statistic and every predicted date is bit-identical. The same maths in `Instant` nanoseconds misses 14 golden lines by more than 1e-6 s — up to 0.0226 s after the roll-forward to Foundation's distant future, and one prediction a whole cycle late where the clock sits exactly on upstream's predicted start — and leaves 45 more dates not bit-identical. | The predicted days the wearer sees, and the cycle the roll-forward lands on, match iOS's to the bit. | — (done; `test/CyclePredictorHazardTest.kt`, `test/EngineDifferentialTest.kt`) |
| D-120 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the cycle predictor, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `CyclePredictor` is an object; the constants are `const val`s in UPPER_SNAKE; upstream's `(night: Date, offsetC: Double)` tuple is `SkinTempNight`. `PeriodEntry` is a data class; `CycleStats`, `CyclePrediction` and `SkinTempNight` are immutable and compare their doubles by IEEE `==`, as Swift's synthesized `Equatable` does; lists are read, never kept or reordered. (2) No ambient zone or clock: the nine functions upstream defaulted to `Calendar.current` take a required `ZoneId`; `predict`'s `now` is required; `skinTempDeviations` and `alreadyCoveredDays` keep upstream's defaults. (3) A mirror day count is a `Long` (Swift's 64-bit `Int`); `alreadyCoveredDays` and `writtenSampleCount` are `Int`s (counts of tracked samples). (4) The day classifiers and mirror bounds work on calendar days (`LocalDate`) in the zone, which agree with Foundation's start-of-day comparisons and day counts across both 2026 New York clock changes (measured), except where midnight is skipped (D-118); `openPeriodAutoExtendLastDay` and `periodMirrorLastDay` return the start of that day. (5) Kept difference, measured: an instant `java.time` cannot place in the zone (`Instant`'s first and last year) is no day — never logged, predicted or ovulation; no last day (`null`), 0 days, never up to date, never capped — where Foundation still reads a day; nothing throws. Foundation's own distant dates agree (1 460 973 days between them). (6) Kept as upstream after measuring: the same skin-temperature night given twice counts twice (the caller passes one value per night); a NaN offset never counts and +∞ does; the ±3-day window is closed; unsorted, duplicated, backwards and overlapping histories; tied starts keep their given order and their durations sum in it; covered-day counts at the `Int` ends (Foundation never answers "no date", so the floor reaches today); today before the start and an end before the start mirror nothing. | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; the zone and the clock are the caller's. | — (done; `test/CyclePredictorGuardTest.kt`, `test/CyclePredictorHazardTest.kt`, `test/CyclePredictorClockChangeTest.kt`) |
| D-121 | **DEVIATION (bound): a detected-workout retention upstream cannot read is bounded.** (1) `CursorSpan.migratedFromLegacyCursor` widens a cursor by `UInt32(max(retention, 0))`, which traps upstream on NaN, an infinity or a value of 2³² or more (measured: "Double value cannot be converted to UInt32"). The port converts as upstream's own `UInt32(clamping:)` does two lines later, after truncation: NaN widens nothing, anything past the top widens by 0xFFFFFFFF. Every other retention gives upstream's answer (measured: −∞ and −5 → no widening, 1.9 → 1, 4 294 967 295.9 → the end wraps to one before the cursor), and the end still wraps past 0xFFFFFFFF, masked to 32 bits as upstream's `&+` wraps (0xFFFFFF00 + 48 h → 172 544). (2) `prunedToRelevant` with a NaN retention compares every span against a NaN horizon upstream and drops them all (measured), so a caller saving the result would erase the dismissed and announced ledger and every reviewed bout would come back. The port keeps every span — the answer upstream's own `AutomaticWorkoutInbox.rebuild` gives on a NaN retention, which keeps every sample (measured: 140 of 140). Every other retention gives upstream's answer. | No retention crashes the inbox's bookkeeping or erases what the wearer already dismissed; the app passes the 48 h constant, so its answers are upstream's. | — (done; `test/WorkoutHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-122 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the workout session and automatic workout detection, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `HRZoneClassifier`, `WorkoutHRBackfill`, `WorkoutBufferedSportFill`, `HistoricalSportFrame`, `AutomaticWorkoutDetector`, `AutomaticWorkoutInbox`, `AutomaticWorkoutAnnouncement` and `AutomaticWorkoutConfirmation` are objects; constants are `const val`s in UPPER_SNAKE (`TimeInterval`s as `Double` seconds). `WorkoutSportType` (10), `HRZone` (raw `Int`) and `SuggestedKind` keep upstream's raw names in declaration order, with `fromRawValue`; `systemImageName` keeps upstream's SF Symbols names verbatim for the app to map. `WorkoutZoneBreakdown`, `WorkoutSummary` and `CursorSpan` are data classes, comparing their doubles by IEEE `==` as Swift's synthesized `Equatable` does; `Sample`, `Candidate`, `Fill`, `State` and `Prepared` copy their lists and bytes in and read them out read-only, and `Fill` is built only by `fill` (upstream's memberwise initializer is internal). (2) `WorkoutSessionAggregator` was already a reference type upstream; it is a mutable class owned by one live workout, with `copy()` for an independent snapshot (samples and the live high-water mark included); `finalize` does not consume it, and `collectedSamples` is a snapshot. (3) Ring cursors (Swift `UInt32`) are `Long`s required in 0…0xFFFFFFFF wherever a `Sample` or `CursorSpan` is built; the window span clamps to that range; the migration's wrap is masked (D-121). A record's step count is an `Int` (a decoded byte, 0…255); every sum of steps — `Candidate.steps`, `Fill.steps`, `WorkoutSummary.steps` and `finalize`'s `steps` — is a `Long` (Swift's 64-bit `Int`), and the bpm sum behind an average is a `Long` whose quotient truncates toward zero, as Swift's `/` does. (4) `AutomaticWorkoutInbox.rebuild` takes a required `now` (upstream defaulted to the device clock); the other defaults are upstream's settings. Where upstream de-duplicates through a Dictionary (`merge`, `detect`, `rebuild`), the port uses an insertion-ordered map and sorts its values on their unique keys, so no iteration order reaches a result; the held-zone sort is stable, as Swift's. (5) Kept differences (the exact-elapsed-time rule): elapsed times are measured exactly between instants, so a window edge 100 ns before a whole second is the previous cursor and a bout 24 h and 1 ns old is past the announcement age, where upstream's `Date` doubles cannot hold the difference. (6) Kept as upstream after measuring: a NaN coverage floor finds no candidate and a NaN hold cap holds every gap uncapped (Swift's `min` / `max` let NaN through); a NaN or infinite inbox retention keeps every sample; a record repeated in the buffered fill is used twice and a stored record's HR is not re-validated (the caller passes the inbox's de-duplicated records); a hand-built breakdown's fractions (−0.5 / 1.5 with a negative field, ∞ / ∞ → NaN, a NaN total → 0); the energy estimate keeps the first of `[HR, distance]` unless the later is greater (a NaN distance estimate never displaces a number); any stored age bounds max HR to 1…219; a hand-built candidate with its end before its start confirms with a negative duration and 0 kcal. | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; the clock is the caller's, and no 32-bit sum or shared buffer changes an answer. | — (done; `test/WorkoutHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-123 | **DEVIATION (bound): a stored workout-recovery snapshot holding a value no session writes is read as no snapshot.** `WorkoutSessionRecovery.decide` returns `NothingToRecover` — upstream's answer for a snapshot it cannot read — for a snapshot whose energy estimate is NaN, infinite or negative, whose reading count is negative, or whose average or maximum heart rate is negative. This is checked before the span refusals, as upstream reads its stored form before it decides. Upstream's own stored form cannot hold a non-finite energy (measured: `encoded()` is nil for NaN, +∞ and −∞, and a stored `"NaN"` does not decode), so end to end upstream never offers one; it does decode and offer negative counts, energy and heart rates as they are (measured: −3 readings, −5 kcal, −104 / −1 bpm). No session writes any of these: the live estimate is a high-water mark starting at 0, and counts and heart rates are never negative. Every value a session writes (−0.0, 0 and 1e308 kcal, no energy, counts 0 … `Int.MAX_VALUE`, heart rate 0) is offered exactly as upstream offers it. | The snapshot is a stored value from a previous process; offering a corrupt one would write its values to the health store, which a later launch cannot take back. Failing closed as "cannot read" keeps upstream's two refusals and their stored names unchanged. | — (done; `test/WorkoutHRGateAndRecoveryHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-124 | **DEFERRED, REQUIREMENT: the workout-recovery snapshot's stored form.** `WorkoutSessionSnapshot.encoded()` / `decoded(from:)` (`S/WorkoutSessionRecovery.swift:76`, `:81` — upstream's JSON in its preferences store) are not ported. **Not ported, named:** `T/WorkoutSessionRecoveryTests.swift` `testSnapshotRoundTripsThroughItsPersistedForm` (`:109`) and `testUnreadableBlobIsTreatedAsAbsent` (`:117`). The decision logic is ported and tested; only the bytes wait. | E6 (`:store`): the snapshot's stored form with a round trip, both named tests against it, and upstream's rule that a blob this build cannot read is no snapshot (`decide(null)`), including a stored reading count past `Int.MAX_VALUE`. |
| D-125 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the live-HR gate and the workout recovery decision, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `WorkoutHRGate` and `WorkoutSessionRecovery` are objects; `DEFAULT_MAX_AGE` is a `const val` of `Double` seconds, and the freshness window is `Double` seconds whose NaN and infinite values keep upstream's meaning. (2) `WorkoutSessionSnapshot` and `RecoveredWorkout` are data classes (`val` + `copy`) comparing their doubles by IEEE `==`, as Swift's synthesized `Equatable` does; `WorkoutRecoveryRefusal` keeps upstream's two raw names in declaration order, with `fromRawValue` matching the exact string; `WorkoutRecoveryDecision` is a sealed interface — `NothingToRecover`, `Discard(refusal)`, `Offer(workout)` (upstream's associated values are unlabelled). (3) `decide` takes a required `now` (upstream defaulted it to the device clock); `shouldRecord`'s `now` is required, as upstream's is. (4) A reading count is an `Int` (Swift's 64-bit `Int`): a live session's count is a list size and always fits; a stored count past 2³¹ is the stored form's concern (D-124). (5) Kept differences (the exact-elapsed-time rule): a lock 6 s and 1 ns old is stale, a heartbeat 1 ns after the start is an observed span and one 1 ns after `now` ends in the future, where upstream's `Date` doubles cannot hold the nanosecond. (6) Kept as upstream after measuring: a lock dated after `now` records (its age is negative); a last recording in the future blocks every real lock until the clock passes it; a session start in the future admits nothing and no session start admits a pre-workout lock; a negative window admits only a lock at least that far ahead, NaN and −∞ admit nothing, +∞ any age; a snapshot both never observed and future-dated is "no observed span" (checked first); a recovered workout built by hand ending before it starts has a negative duration. | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; the clock is the caller's. | — (done; `test/WorkoutHRGateAndRecoveryHazardTest.kt`, `test/WorkoutGuardTest.kt`) |
| D-126 | **DEVIATION (improvement): an alarm time inside a clock change that is not a whole hour stays on its day.** The alarm's wall-clock time is resolved by one helper, `CalendarDay.atWallClock`, which reproduces Foundation's `date(bySettingHour:minute:second:of:)` as measured on the pinned build over every minute of both 2026 changing days in New York, Adelaide, Lord Howe and Santiago: a time the clock shows once is that instant; one it shows twice is the first; one it skips is the end of the gap (New York's 02:30 on 8 March → 03:00, where `java.time`'s own rule gives 03:30); an hour outside 0…23 or a minute outside 0…59 is no time; the answer depends only on the date. The one difference: Lord Howe moves its clock 30 minutes (02:00 → 02:30, 4 October 2026), and Foundation resolves 02:00–02:29 that day to the same time the NEXT day — so upstream's 02:15 alarm never sounds that morning (its most recent occurrence stays the day before's, its next is the day after's). The port resolves it to 02:30 that day, the end of the gap, as Foundation does for every whole-hour gap. | Upstream's own test asks that a time the clock skips resolve to a real instant "rather than vanishing for the day"; Foundation does so for whole-hour gaps and not for this one. Every other time in every zone is unchanged (swept over every gap and overlap of every zone the JVM knows in 2026–27). | — (done; `test/RingAlarmAndProximityHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-127 | **DEVIATION (improvement): the proximity dial is empty for a non-negative RSSI.** `RingProximity.signalFraction` returns 0.0 for a reading of 0 or more, where upstream clamps it to −45 dBm and returns 1.0 (measured at 0, 1, 127 and the `Int` maximum). 127 is the Bluetooth "RSSI not available" value, and `band` already reads every non-negative reading as "searching". Every negative reading keeps upstream's value. | Upstream draws a full signal dial beside the label "Searching…". The dial is now empty whenever the band is "searching" and stays within 0…1 (swept −2 000…2 000 and the `Int` ends). | — (done; `test/RingAlarmAndProximityHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-128 | **DEVIATION: API shape of the ring alarm and ring proximity, and outcomes kept as upstream.** (1) `RingAlarm` is an immutable class (`val` + `copy`) whose weekday set is copied in and read-only (ascending); it compares `burstSpacing` by IEEE `==`, as Swift's synthesized `Equatable`; `hour`, `minute` and `burstCount` are `Int`s (a stored value past 32 bits is the stored form's concern, D-129). `RingAlarmDecision` is a sealed interface — `Fire(scheduled, lateBy)` (`lateBy` in `Double` seconds, compared by IEEE `==`), `Missed(scheduled)`, `Idle`. `RingAlarmBlock` keeps upstream's four raw names in declaration order with `fromRawValue` (exact match); it is used only by the app's alarm controller but is ported here. `RingAlarmSchedule` and `RingProximity` are objects; the constants are `const val`s in UPPER_SNAKE (`TimeInterval`s as `Double` seconds); `Band` is an enum (no raw names upstream) whose `label`s are verbatim. Swift's argument labels are dropped (`repeats(weekday)`, `mostRecentOccurrence(now, alarm, zone)`, `band(rssi)`). (2) No ambient zone or clock: the four rules upstream defaulted to the device's calendar (`warmUpTarget`, `mostRecentOccurrence`, `nextOccurrence`, `decide`) take a required `ZoneId`; `now` was already required; the warm-up window and the grace keep upstream's defaults. (3) Days are walked by calendar date in the zone; a weekday is Foundation's number (1 = Sunday); `lateBy` and the warm-up window are measured exactly between instants (the elapsed-time rule of the earlier slices). (4) `approximateMeters` uses `StrictMath.pow` (the engine differential: 18 of the distances are one ulp from upstream's platform `pow`, every text identical); `distanceText` rounds as Swift's `rounded()` and formats nothing through the default locale. (5) Kept difference: an instant `java.time` cannot place in the zone (`Instant`'s first and last year) has no occurrence and the decision is idle, where upstream's `Date` reaches it and its calendar answers; nothing throws. Foundation's distant dates and both sides of 1582's switch to the Julian calendar agree exactly (measured). (6) Kept as upstream after measuring: a weekday outside 1…7 is taken literally, so a set holding only such days never fires; NaN, negative and −∞ grace never fire (missed) and +∞ always fires; a NaN, non-positive or −∞ warm-up never arms, and +∞ arms on every next occurrence; a "last handled" stamp at or after the occurrence holds it, even one in the future; a NaN burst spacing stays NaN (upstream's stored form cannot hold one); the burst count clamps to 1…10 at every `Int`. | Upstream's answers for every input the API accepts; the zone and the clock are the caller's. | — (done; `test/RingAlarmAndProximityGuardTest.kt`, `test/RingAlarmAndProximityHazardTest.kt`) |
| D-129 | **DEFERRED, REQUIREMENT: the ring alarm's stored form.** Upstream persists `RingAlarm` as JSON in its preferences store (`Codable`); the port has no stored form. **Not ported, named:** `T/RingAlarmTests.swift` `roundTripsThroughJSON` (`:174`). | `:ringkit` has no serialization dependency and the storage epic owns every stored form (the rule D-10 set). The scheduling rules are ported and tested; only the bytes wait. | E6 (`:store`): the alarm's stored form with a round trip (the named test against it), keeping upstream's rule that an unreadable stored alarm must not silently reset the user's alarm, and deciding how a stored hour, minute or burst count past 32 bits is read. |
| D-130 | **DEVIATION: no main file reads an ambient clock, time zone, locale, preferences store or the environment — the audit covers the whole module.** The source audit reads every main file, listed from the source directory (113 at E5a's close), where it read a hand-kept list of the files E4 added or changed. Measured when it was widened: one ambient read outside that list — `Command.syncUpToNow` defaulted `now` to the device clock (`Instant.now()`, as upstream's `Date()`) — so `now` is now required; every caller already passed it. Five hits that are not ambient reads are excused by name, each with its reason: the debug `toString`s of `BulkRecord`, `EpochRecord.ActivityRecord` (and its hex helper) and `Frame.Parsed` format a literal in the default locale, but only with lowercase `%x` and `%s`, which java.util.Formatter never localizes — the audit pins the same ASCII text under Arabic, Devanagari, Thai and Persian digits and Turkish case rules. An exception that no longer matches exactly one hit fails the audit. Preferences-store reads join the rules. | The rule each port set for its own files, carried to every file, with no list to forget: the same inputs give the same answer on every machine, in every zone and language. | — (done; `test/NoAmbientEnvironmentTest.kt`) |
